PR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/60369
Reviewed-By: Colin Ihrig <cjihrig@gmail.com>

| [benchmark](https://github.com/nodejs/node/tree/main/benchmark "benchmark")  | [benchmark](https://github.com/nodejs/node/tree/main/benchmark "benchmark")  | [fs: watch directories, not files, in recursive fs.watch fallback](https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/2ec0f9ac60c58fd48fb7983cc9eb9069259289ac "fs: watch directories, not files, in recursive fs.watch fallback

The JavaScript recursive watcher used on platforms without a native one
\(notably Linux\) armed an fs.watch\(\) handle and a stat\(\) for every file
in the tree, and answered every event by stat\(\)ing and re-reading the
whole directory it happened in. A 13k-entry tree cost 13.5k inotify
watches, ~150 ms and ~50 MB to set up, and appending to one file in a
3900-entry directory cost ~9 ms of CPU per event. A file replaced by
rename\(\) \(the usual editor save\) also stopped being reported, since its
watch stayed on the old inode.

inotify reports changes to the entries of a watched directory, with
their names, so on Linux watch each directory once \(symbolic links keep
their own watcher, as before\), keep the set of known paths, and resolve
an event with a single stat\(\) of the named entry: unknown names are
added and reported as 'rename', vanished ones are dropped and reported
as 'rename', file changes are reported as 'change'. kqueue and event
ports only report that a directory changed, so on the other platforms
served by this fallback every file keeps its own watcher and a
directory event rescans that directory, as before. unref\(\) and ref\(\)
now reach the underlying handles.

Signed-off-by: Shelley Vohr <shelley.vohr@gmail.com>

PR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/65597
Reviewed-By: Luigi Pinca <luigipinca@gmail.com>
Reviewed-By: Beth Griggs <bethanyngriggs@gmail.com>")  | Aug 31, 2026  |
| [lib](https://github.com/nodejs/node/tree/main/lib "lib")  | [lib](https://github.com/nodejs/node/tree/main/lib "lib")  | [tls: read the peer certificate chain without consuming it](https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/705646f2f70fd8097d13d85de8347a74b6c443af "tls: read the peer certificate chain without consuming it

X509Certificate::GetPeerCert\(\) built the certificate objects by deleting
entries from the stack returned by SSL_get_peer_cert_chain\(\), which is
owned by the SSL session. The first read emptied it, so any later read
by getPeerCertificate\(\) or getPeerX509Certificate\(\), on either peer, saw
a truncated chain or nothing. Copy each issuer with X509_dup instead and
leave the session's stack untouched.

onServerSocketSecure\(\) only called getPeerX509Certificate\(\) to check
whether a peer certificate was present, building the whole chain on
every server handshake; that is what first exposed the destructive read.
Use a lightweight hasPeerCertificate\(\) binding for the presence check.

Signed-off-by: Tony Gies <tgies@tgies.net>

| [LICENSE](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/LICENSE "LICENSE")  | [LICENSE](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/LICENSE "LICENSE")  | [build,src: make --use-largepages a no-op](https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/15940ef908f992f4bd754d0dce882fd66f2550bc "build,src: make --use-largepages a no-op

This feature has not been actively used in the wild and has
always only been best-effort on systems that support it. The build
changes it needs breaks WSL1 compatibility and the relocation
mechanism won't work with newer V8 that remaps builtins using
mremap\(MREMAP_DONTUNMAP\).

into no-ops. A warning will be printed if `--use-largepages=on`
is used.

| [SECURITY.md](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/SECURITY.md "SECURITY.md")  | [SECURITY.md](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/SECURITY.md "SECURITY.md")  | [meta: refine the security vuln posture for experimental features](https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/c66ae4f2cbf36eea1952ed491210b9fb3a5d7079 "meta: refine the security vuln posture for experimental features

Currently, any runtime gated experimental feature security
vulnerability is considered a "valid" vulnerability, even if
the work is still very much work in progress. This adds friction
because it ends up requiring that things be compile time gated
which makes it more difficult if there is not a corresponding
CI job to run tests.

This relaxes the policy a bit. For anything that is 1.0 and 1.1,
a vuln report is only valid if it directly compromises a stable
feature, allows a workaround that compromises a stable feature,
etc. An example would be an obvious DOS vector that can be
exploited even if the feature is not enabled, for instance.

This gives us leeway to reject vuln reports on things that are
enabled only by `--experimental-*` runtime flag that are still
works in progress.

Given it's complexity and active development, give ourselves
coverage by specifically excluding QUIC/H3 from vuln reports.
Any vuln reports received for QUIC, unless it's something that
can compromise security of something else in Node.js when the
`--experimental-quic` runtime flag is not enabled, should be
handled as regular issues and not as security vulns

unofficial.gni special-cased ia32 on current_cpu while every other
architecture used target_cpu. When node is also compiled in a host
toolchain of a cross build \(for example node_mksnapshot running in
V8's snapshot toolchain, which is x86-hosted for 32-bit arm targets\),
current_cpu is the host's, so NODE_ARCH became "ia32" for an
arm-targeted build and process.arch deserialized as 'ia32' on the
device. Key the special case off target_cpu like the rest.

# Node.js
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#nodejs)
Node.js is an open-source, cross-platform JavaScript runtime environment.
For information on using Node.js, see the [Node.js website](https://nodejs.org/).
The Node.js project uses an [open governance model](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/GOVERNANCE.md). The [OpenJS Foundation](https://openjsf.org/) provides support for the project.
Contributors are expected to act in a collaborative manner to move the project forward. We encourage the constructive exchange of contrary opinions and compromise. The [TSC](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/GOVERNANCE.md#technical-steering-committee) reserves the right to limit or block contributors who repeatedly act in ways that discourage, exhaust, or otherwise negatively affect other participants.
**This project has a[Code of Conduct](https://github.com/nodejs/admin/blob/HEAD/CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md).**
## Table of contents
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#table-of-contents)

    * [Download](https://github.com/nodejs/node#download)

    * [Release keys](https://github.com/nodejs/node#release-keys)
  * [License](https://github.com/nodejs/node#license)


## Support
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#support)
Looking for help? Check out the [instructions for getting support](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/.github/SUPPORT.md).
## Release types
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#release-types)
  * **Current** : Under active development. Code for the Current release is in the branch for its major version number (for example, [v22.x](https://github.com/nodejs/node/tree/v22.x)). Node.js releases a new major version every 6 months, allowing for breaking changes. This happens in April and October every year. Releases appearing each October have a support life of 8 months. Releases appearing each April convert to LTS (see below) each October.
  * **LTS** : Releases that receive Long Term Support, with a focus on stability and security. Every even-numbered major version will become an LTS release. LTS releases receive 12 months of _Active LTS_ support and a further 18 months of _Maintenance_. LTS release lines have alphabetically-ordered code names, beginning with v4 Argon. There are no breaking changes or feature additions, except in some special circumstances.
  * **Nightly** : Code from the Current branch built every 24-hours when there are changes. Use with caution.


Current and LTS releases follow [semantic versioning](https://semver.org). A member of the Release Team [signs](https://github.com/nodejs/node#release-keys) each Current and LTS release. For more information, see the [Release README](https://github.com/nodejs/Release#readme).
### Download
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#download)
Binaries, installers, and source tarballs are available at <https://nodejs.org/en/download/>.
#### Current and LTS releases
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#current-and-lts-releases)
<https://nodejs.org/download/release/>
The [latest](https://nodejs.org/download/release/latest/) directory is an alias for the latest Current release. The latest-_codename_ directory is an alias for the latest release from an LTS line. For example, the [latest-hydrogen](https://nodejs.org/download/release/latest-hydrogen/) directory contains the latest Hydrogen (Node.js 18) release.
#### Nightly releases
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#nightly-releases)
<https://nodejs.org/download/nightly/>
Each directory and filename includes the version (e.g., `v22.0.0`), followed by the UTC date (e.g., `20240424` for April 24, 2024), and the short commit SHA of the HEAD of the release (e.g., `ddd0a9e494`). For instance, a full directory name might look like `v22.0.0-nightly20240424ddd0a9e494`.
#### API documentation
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#api-documentation)
Documentation for the latest Current release is at <https://nodejs.org/api/>. Version-specific documentation is available in each release directory in the _docs_ subdirectory. Version-specific documentation is also at <https://nodejs.org/download/docs/>.
### Verifying binaries
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#verifying-binaries)
Download directories contain a `SHASUMS256.txt.asc` file with SHA checksums for the files and the releaser PGP signature.
You can get a trusted keyring from nodejs/release-keys, e.g. using `curl`:

```
curl -fsLo "/path/to/nodejs-keyring.kbx" "https://github.com/nodejs/release-keys/raw/HEAD/gpg/pubring.kbx"
```

Alternatively, you can import the releaser keys in your default keyring, see [Release keys](https://github.com/nodejs/node#release-keys) for commands on how to do that.
Then, you can verify the files you've downloaded locally (if you're using your default keyring, pass `--keyring="${GNUPGHOME:-~/.gnupg}/pubring.kbx"`):

```
curl -fsO "https://nodejs.org/dist/${VERSION}/SHASUMS256.txt.asc" \
&& gpgv --keyring="/path/to/nodejs-keyring.kbx" --output SHASUMS256.txt < SHASUMS256.txt.asc \
&& shasum --check SHASUMS256.txt --ignore-missing
```

### Security release stewards
[](https://github.com/nodejs/node#security-release-stewards)
When possible, the commitment to take slots in the security release steward rotation is made by companies in order to ensure individuals who act as security stewards have the support and recognition from their employer to be able to prioritize security releases. Security release stewards manage security releases on a rotation basis as outlined in the [security release process](https://github.com/nodejs/node/blob/main/doc/contributing/security-release-process.md).

3. 程序判断（不是最终结论）

调用 IDcrawl4ai__live-020-navigation-menu__r001
HTTP 状态200
最终 URLhttps://github.com/nodejs/node
输出大小96,321 字符
事实覆盖率（核对点命中率）100.0%
最小长度通过

评分核对点逐条判定

每个核对点对应标准答案中一项必须出现的事实；可接受表达命中任意一个即可。

核对点程序状态可接受表达实际命中
anchor-1命中nodejs/nodenodejs/node
anchor-2命中Node.js JavaScript runtimeNode.js JavaScript runtime
anchor-3命中Table of contentsTable of contents

显著标记：预期 / 实际命中

nodejs/node

    {
      "href": "https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/2ec0f9ac60c58fd48fb7983cc9eb9069259289ac",
      "text": "fs: watch directories, not files, in recursive fs.watch fallback",
      "title": "fs: watch directories, not files, in recursive fs.watch fallback\n\nThe JavaScript recursive watcher used on platforms without a native one\n(notably Linux) armed an fs.watch() handle and a stat() for every file\nin the tree, and answered every event by stat()ing and re-reading the\nwhole directory it happened in. A 13k-entry tree cost 13.5k inotify\nwatches, ~150 ms and ~50 MB to set up, and appending to one file in a\n3900-entry directory cost ~9 ms of CPU per event. A file replaced by\nrename() (the usual editor save) also stopped being reported, since its\nwatch stayed on the old inode.\n\ninotify reports changes to the entries of a watched directory, with\ntheir names, so on Linux watch each directory once (symbolic links keep\ntheir own watcher, as before), keep the set of known paths, and resolve\nan event with a single stat() of the named entry: unknown names are\nadded and reported as 'rename', vanished ones are dropped and reported\nas 'rename', file changes are reported as 'change'. kqueue and event\nports only report that a directory changed, so on the other platforms\nserved by this fallback every file keeps its own watcher and a\ndirectory event rescans that directory, as before. unref() and ref()\nnow reach the underlying handles.\n\nSigned-off-by: Shelley Vohr <shelley.vohr@gmail.com>\nPR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/65486\nReviewed-By: Moshe Atlow <moshe@atlow.co.il>",

      "href": "https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/705646f2f70fd8097d13d85de8347a74b6c443af",
      "text": "tls: read the peer certificate chain without consuming it",
      "title": "tls: read the peer certificate chain without consuming it\n\nX509Certificate::GetPeerCert() built the certificate objects by deleting\nentries from the stack returned by SSL_get_peer_cert_chain(), which is\nowned by the SSL session. The first read emptied it, so any later read\nby getPeerCertificate() or getPeerX509Certificate(), on either peer, saw\na truncated chain or nothing. Copy each issuer with X509_dup instead and\nleave the session's stack untouched.\n\nonServerSocketSecure() only called getPeerX509Certificate() to check\nwhether a peer certificate was present, building the whole chain on\nevery server handshake; that is what first exposed the destructive read.\nUse a lightweight hasPeerCertificate() binding for the presence check.\n\nSigned-off-by: Tony Gies <tgies@tgies.net>\nPR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/65602\nFixes: https://github.com/nodejs/node/issues/65579\nRefs: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/64677\nReviewed-By: Filip Skokan <panva.ip@gmail.com>\nReviewed-By: Tim Perry <pimterry@gmail.com>",

      "href": "https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/c66ae4f2cbf36eea1952ed491210b9fb3a5d7079",
      "text": "meta: refine the security vuln posture for experimental features",
      "title": "meta: refine the security vuln posture for experimental features\n\nCurrently, any runtime gated experimental feature security\nvulnerability is considered a \"valid\" vulnerability, even if\nthe work is still very much work in progress. This adds friction\nbecause it ends up requiring that things be compile time gated\nwhich makes it more difficult if there is not a corresponding\nCI job to run tests.\n\nThis relaxes the policy a bit. For anything that is 1.0 and 1.1,\na vuln report is only valid if it directly compromises a stable\nfeature, allows a workaround that compromises a stable feature,\netc. An example would be an obvious DOS vector that can be\nexploited even if the feature is not enabled, for instance.\n\nThis gives us leeway to reject vuln reports on things that are\nenabled only by `--experimental-*` runtime flag that are still\nworks in progress.\n\nGiven it's complexity and active development, give ourselves\ncoverage by specifically excluding QUIC/H3 from vuln reports.\nAny vuln reports received for QUIC, unless it's something that\ncan compromise security of something else in Node.js when the\n`--experimental-quic` runtime flag is not enabled, should be\nhandled as regular issues and not as security vulns\n\nSigned-off-by: James M Snell <jasnell@gmail.com>\nPR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/65438\nReviewed-By: Matteo Collina <matteo.collina@gmail.com>\nReviewed-By: Marco Ippolito <marcoippolito54@gmail.com>\nReviewed-By: Rafael Gonzaga <rafael.nunu@hotmail.com>\nReviewed-By: Beth Griggs <bethanyngriggs@gmail.com>\nReviewed-By: Gürgün Dayıoğlu <hey@gurgun.day>\nReviewed-By: Chengzhong Wu <legendecas@gmail.com>",

      "href": "https://github.com/nodejs/node/commit/fc452effb6cfed98704f45d2c1f70bd580d805a7",
      "text": "build: derive NODE_ARCH from target_cpu in the GN build",
      "title": "build: derive NODE_ARCH from target_cpu in the GN build\n\nunofficial.gni special-cased ia32 on current_cpu while every other\narchitecture used target_cpu. When node is also compiled in a host\ntoolchain of a cross build (for example node_mksnapshot running in\nV8's snapshot toolchain, which is x86-hosted for 32-bit arm targets),\ncurrent_cpu is the host's, so NODE_ARCH became \"ia32\" for an\narm-targeted build and process.arch deserialized as 'ia32' on the\ndevice. Key the special case off target_cpu like the rest.\n\nSigned-off-by: Shelley Vohr <shelley.vohr@gmail.com>\nPR-URL: https://github.com/nodejs/node/pull/65491\nRefs: https://github.com/electron/electron/pull/52959\nReviewed-By: Filip Skokan <panva.ip@gmail.com>",

4. 人工二次复核（HIL）

请先并排阅读标准答案与完整输出，再独立作出最终判断。人工结论可以推翻机器初判，但必须填写理由。
